[THINKING]
SMTP: many external messages with random HELO, random mail_from/rcpt_to, random attachments. Looks like a spam run with randomly generated attachments. Deployed rules have "ET HUNTING SUSPICIOUS SMTP EXE - EXE SMTP Attachment" etc but only 3 fired for .exe attachment? There is one .apk and one .exe in fileinfo; .exe likely fired. But many suspicious file types (.xls, .doc, .ppt, .rtf, .m3u, .wri) could be malware. But random attachments with odd extensions may be generated benign traffic? We need not detect ordinary SMTP from outside: legitimate external users sending mail to internal server is normal. Random HELO domains could be spam, but not necessarily attack; SMTP servers accept mail from random domains. Could be open relay test/spam? Hard to write low FP.

However, SMTP events may include suspicious MIME long line anomalies (8404) and deployed rules perhaps no alert. But MIME_LONG_LINE likely indicates very long lines in email body; could be buffer overflow attempt? We can inspect anomaly events with MIME_LONG_LINE and associated SMTP flow. Stack smtp fields for anomalies? Anomaly events don't have smtp. But query anomaly MIME_LONG_LINE samples.

--- Turn 31 | 1,342,209 in / 12,088 out ---

[THINKING]
SMTP external emails are clearly spam-like with random sender/recipient addresses and attachments. But legitimate external mail can be from arbitrary domains. Detection of "random" is hard without baselining. However there are specific suspicious email.url with embedded binary junk, and one email body with `echo. Existing rules maybe detect SMTP EXE. Not a good path.

Let's look at HTTP more carefully; likely best missed attacks. Also FTP and SMB.

Let's count FTP commands and command_data rare, alerts.

[THINKING]
FTP command_data rare shows format strings, long buffers, numeric boundary values, path traversal, etc. These are likely fuzzing/overflow attempts. Deployed rules already fired many FTP overflow alerts (GPL FTP command overflow, USER overflow, format string, LIST overflow) but maybe missed specific command_data (not only USER). Count_alerts: GPL FTP command overflow 94, USER overflow 26/20, format string 6/7, LIST overflow 3, etc. However command_data rare includes numeric boundary sequences like `1048574,...`, `4294967295,...` — probably FTP fuzzing. Could write rules for FTP commands with numeric overflow/format strings? But deployed already has many. Need find specific missed attack.

Maybe focus on SIP/MQTT/DNS/WebDAV. Let's examine SMB and dcerpc. Event_type smb 321, dcerpc 52. Stack smb.command? Query sample.

Let's count how many HTTP URLs contain percent-encoded XSS indicators. Use jq `select(.event_type=="http") | select(.http.url // "" | test("%3c.*(EmbeD|iFrame|script|OnMouSEOver|alert)|%3C.*(EmbeD|iFrame|script|OnMouSEOver|alert)"))`. But jq regex case? Suricata uses content matching on normalized? We can write content rule with `http.uri; content:"%3C%3CEmbeD"; nocase;` etc. Need see if Suricata normalizes percent encodings in http.uri sticky buffer? The `http.uri` buffer is raw URI or normalized? Suricata normalizes some, but likely raw as sent. Test_rule will tell.

But maybe better generic rule: detect HTTP URI containing `%3Cscript`, `%3Cembed`, `%3Ciframe` followed by `javascript:` (encoded or not). Could generate many alerts (probably many XSS attempts). - HTTP: many events, alerts concentrated; specific missed: obfuscated XSS, directory traversal, WebDAV PROPFIND DLL.
- LDAP: 7 events, 1 alert, and one CRAM-MD5 bind with oversized spaces.
- FTP/SSH/POP3/SMTP looked noisy but attacks already partially covered or not reliably separable from legitimate.

Hypotheses:
1. DNS BIND version/authors TXT query = recon.
2. DNS AXFR requests over UDP/TCP = zone transfer attempt; baseline only alerted TCP AXFR? Actually count_alerts showed TCP alerts only 6; our rule detects rrtype 252 request (4). Good.
3. WebDAV PROPFIND MiniRedir for .dll = remote DLL retrieval/recon against web servers.
4. Encoded mixed-case XSS with javascript URI = web app XSS.
5. Directory traversal: repeated ../ and encoded/raw backslashes = file disclosure attempts.
6. LDAP SASL CRAM-MD5 bind with 40 spaces = oversized credential/fuzzing/possible overflow.

Rules saved with test_rule numbers:
- sid 7900001 PASS (2 alerts external->internal, no internal-internal)
- 7900002 PASS (2)
- 7900003 PASS (4)
- 7900004 PASS (5)
- 7900008 PASS (6)
- 7900006 PASS (9)
- 7900012 PASS (2)
- 7900013 PASS (1)
- 7900010 PASS (1)

Rules dropped:
- 7900005 FAIL no alerts (used http.uri `%3C` raw in normalized buffer) replaced by 7900008 using normalized `<`.
- 7900007 FAIL no alerts (used http.uri.raw `%3C` with subsequent relative contents maybe raw order) replaced by 7900008.
- 7900009 PASS but superseded by stricter 7900010 (the first raw-space rule widened to 10 alerts because it matched any port; stricter CRAM-MD5+spaces kept 1 alert and tighter).
- 7900011 FAIL no alerts (normalized http.uri backslash content didn't match; raw rule 7900012/7900013 used instead).

Activity believed attack but couldn't write reliable rule:
- POP3 from external with random usernames/passwords all successful; looked like credential stuffing but all sessions authenticated and retrieved mail, so no reliable malicious-vs-legitimate discriminator.
- SMTP random HELO/sender/recipient with random attachments from external; spam-like but normal external mail can have arbitrary domains/attachments. We didn't write a rule.
- FTP command_data fuzzing with boundary numbers/format strings mostly already covered by deployed FTP rules; remaining rare numeric sequences too generic/lab-specific.
- SIP INVITE/CANCEL/BYE traffic mostly already had INVITE flood and shellcode/overflow alerts; remaining looked like normal SIP calls.
- SSH was all internal client-to-server, no external brute force.
- FTP-data and fileinfo transfers were internal and ordinary.

## Coverage map

Counts from `logs/unsw-3-baseline/eve.json`.

**Alerts per app_proto** — filter: `select(.event_type=="alert") | .app_proto // "none"`

- none 2948, http 912, smtp 760, ftp 469, failed 284, smb 179, tftp 52, dcerpc 27, snmp 26, dns 20, sip 11, mqtt 8, imap 6, dhcp 2, ssh 1, ldap 1.

The gaps I hunted were: **DNS** (35131 events, only 20 alerts), **HTTP** (large volume but specific obfuscated/traversal activity missed), **LDAP** (7 events, 1 alert), plus FTP/SSH/POP3/SMTP/FTP-data which I checked and mostly found either normal internal traffic or activity already covered.

## Hypotheses and findings

1. **DNS BIND `VERSION.BIND` / `AUTHORS.BIND` TXT queries**
   - Rare DNS rrnames were `VERSION.BIND` and `AUTHORS.BIND` with `rrtype:"TXT"` from external hosts to internal DNS servers.
   - `search_rules` found no loaded rule for `VERSION.BIND`.
   - This is classic BIND version/info reconnaissance.

2. **DNS AXFR requests**
   - DNS `rrtype:252` appeared as AXFR requests over both UDP and TCP. The baseline fired only the TCP zone-transfer alert, so the AXFR query itself was missed.
   - Filter used: `select(.event_type=="dns") | (.dns.queries[0].rrtype // .dns.rrtype)`.

3. **WebDAV `PROPFIND` for Windows DLLs**
   - External hosts using `Microsoft-WebDAV-MiniRedir/5.1.2600` sent `PROPFIND` for paths like `/pp7x32.dll`, `/rpawinet.dll`, `/GroovePerfmon.dll`, `/ipl.dll`.
   - Existing `ET HUNTING WebDAV Retrieving .dll` did not fire on these flows; the deployed rule set missed them.

4. **Obfuscated / mixed-case encoded XSS**
   - HTTP URLs contained percent-decoded `<` plus `JavaScrIpt` / `javAsCRiPt` and `alert(...)` in `EMBED`, `IFRAME`, `script` payloads.
   - The three `EmbeD` flows and the mixed-case `iFrame` flows had no matching alerts.
   - The existing `ET WEB_SERVER Script tag in URI` and `Onmouseover=` rules caught some simpler variants, but missed these mixed-case `JavaScrIpt` payloads.

5. **HTTP directory traversal**
   - Many URLs carried repeated `../` traversal, raw `..\..\..` traversal, and percent-encoded `..%5c..%5c` traversal.
   - Several flows, e.g. `/imc/tmp/syslog/download?fileName=../../../../...`, `/BPELConsole/script?../../...`, and `..%5c` variants, had no deployed alert.

6. **LDAP SASL CRAM-MD5 bind with oversized space padding**
   - An LDAP bind request used SASL mechanism `CRAM-MD5` and a credential value of dozens/hundreds of consecutive `0x20` spaces.
   - `search_rules` found no loaded rule for `CRAM-MD5`.
   - This is consistent with LDAP bind fuzzing / oversized credential padding, not normal LDAP.

## Rules saved

Appended to `rules/generated/unsw/rounds_hunt2.rules`. All passed `test_rule` against `data/pcaps/unsw/3.pcap`.

| sid | Rule | test_rule result |
|---:|---|---|
| 7900001 | DNS BIND version.bind TXT query | PASS — 2 alerts ext→int, 0 int→int |
| 7900002 | DNS BIND authors.bind TXT query | PASS — 2 alerts ext→int, 0 int→int |
| 7900003 | DNS AXFR zone transfer request (`dns.rrtype:252`) | PASS — 4 alerts ext→int, 0 int→int |
| 7900004 | WebDAV PROPFIND for Windows DLL | PASS — 5 alerts ext→int, 0 int→int |
| 7900008 | Encoded obfuscated XSS with JavaScript URI | PASS — 6 alerts ext→int, 0 int→int |
| 7900006 | HTTP URI directory traversal (`../../../../`) | PASS — 9 alerts ext→int, 0 int→int |
| 7900012 | HTTP URI percent-encoded backslash traversal (`%5c`) | PASS — 2 alerts ext→int, 0 int→int |
| 7900013 | HTTP URI raw backslash traversal (`..\..\`) | PASS — 1 alert ext→int, 0 int→int |
| 7900010 | LDAP SASL CRAM-MD5 bind with oversized space padding | PASS — 1 alert ext→int, 0 int→int |

## Rules dropped

| sid | Reason |
|---|---|
| 7900005 | FAIL, 0 alerts. Used `http.uri` with literal `%3C`; the normalized URI buffer had already decoded it. Replaced by 7900008 using `<`. |
| 7900007 | FAIL, 0 alerts. Used `http.uri.raw` with `%3C` and relative contents that did not match this capture. Replaced by 7900008. |
| 7900011 | FAIL, 0 alerts. Used normalized `http.uri` for backslash traversal; the raw backslash bytes are only visible in `http.uri.raw`. Replaced by 7900012/7900013. |
| 7900009 | PASS but superseded by stricter 7900010. The raw 40-space rule widened to 10 alerts on any port; adding `CRAM-MD5` plus the spaces kept only the LDAP bind and widened to 1 alert. |

## Activity I believe is attack-like but did not write a rule for

- **POP3 random credential sessions** — 50 external sessions all used random-looking `USER`/`PASS` and all succeeded, so there was no failure/brute-force signal to separate attack from legitimate remote mail retrieval.
- **SMTP random HELO/sender/recipient + random attachments** — strongly spam-like, but ordinary external mail can legitimately use arbitrary domains and attachments; a reliable non-lab-specific discriminator was not clear.
- **FTP command-data fuzzing** — many `%n`, numeric boundary, and long-buffer values existed, but the remaining activity was either already covered by the deployed FTP overflow/format-string rules or too generic to separate from normal FTP use.
- **SIP INVITE/CANCEL/BYE traffic** — the flood/overflow alerts already covered the malicious subset; the rest looked like normal SIP signaling from external callers.
- **SSH** — all 1225 SSH events were internal clients to internal servers, no external brute force or malformed banner activity.
